Since that project started being mine, basically.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or find it difficult to manage your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I wouldn’t have called them “mood swings” at first, because it didn’t feel like I was swinging randomly. It felt like the lab was finally moving at the speed it should have been moving all along. Like everything was lit up, almost electrified, and I could see what needed to happen so clearly.\n\nBut the emotional part has been hard to manage. For months now I’ve had this feeling of being intensely driven, like there’s this momentum and everyone should just get on board. And then if someone questions the plan, or asks whether we should slow down, or wants another meeting before acting, I can feel myself get irritated so fast. Not just mildly annoyed — I snap. I cut people off, or my tone gets sharp, and I’ll say things in a way that makes it sound like they’re being stupid for not seeing what I see.\n\nAnd I don’t like admitting that. I’ve noticed students avoiding my office, or hesitating before they bring something to me, like they’re bracing for how I’ll react. That feels awful, because I don’t want to be someone people are afraid to talk to. But in the moment, when they’re slowing things down, it feels almost unbearable. Like they’re dragging the whole lab backward.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personal relationships since you started experiencing these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly at work, though it feels personal because the lab has been such a big part of my life and identity.\n\nThings have changed with people who used to trust me without much second-guessing. My department has had to step in on decisions I normally would have handled myself — scheduling, priorities, who needed to do what and when. I used to be the person who kept all of that straight, or at least I thought I was. Lately I’ve missed things that should have been obvious, or I’ll focus hard on one detail and lose track of the larger priority, and then the schedule gets confused or people are waiting on me for direction.\n\nAnd it’s not just embarrassing, it’s strained the relationships. My students and staff don’t come to me as easily, and my colleagues have started checking behind me in ways they didn’t used to. I can tell they’re not just being helpful; they’re worried the project could get off track, or that I might miss something connected to lab safety expectations. That’s… hard to say out loud. I’ve always taken safety and supervision seriously.\n\nSo yes, the relationships have changed. There’s less trust, I think. More caution around me. And I hate that, because these are people I’ve worked with for years, and now it feels like the department is having to protect the project from my mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced similar difficulties with focus, restlessness, or managing your emotions in your personal life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve been thinking about that, because it’s hard to separate “personal life” from work right now. Work has sort of expanded into everything — my evenings, weekends, what I’m thinking about when I’m supposed to be doing something else — so when you ask outside of work, I don’t have a clean answer.\n\nI haven’t noticed it in the same concrete way, if that makes sense. At work there are obvious examples: missed scheduling things, getting irritated with people, jumping between tasks. At home or outside the lab, there isn’t really the same structure where I can point to it and say, “There, that’s the same problem.” I don’t know if that means it isn’t happening, or if I’m just less aware of it because there aren’t students and colleagues reflecting it back at me.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clear yes the way I can with work. Most of what I’ve noticed has been tied to the project and the lab.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing everyday tasks at home, like chores or personal projects, due to your difficulty with focus or restlessness?\n\nPatient: At home… I’m not sure it shows up as “chores” in the ordinary way, like dishes or laundry, because I haven’t been paying very good attention to those categories lately. The clearest version of it is still tied to the lab, even when I’m not physically there.\n\nThe reason I came in is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call, or in a safety meeting where I really do need to be tracking the main point, and then I’ll notice something — like a pump vibration in the background, or a student making some side comment, or I’ll remember some odd peak from an old spectrum — and suddenly that becomes the most important thing in the world. I’ll go chasing it. Not just making a note to check later, but actually losing hours to it, digging through old files or trying to connect it to something else, and meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me just… falls apart.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether everyday tasks get completed, I’d say the problem is more that whatever I’m supposed to be doing gets displaced. The thing in front of me gets hijacked by some other detail that feels urgent or meaningful. And by the time I come back to what I was supposed to finish, I’ve either missed the window or someone else has had to patch it together.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and restlessness present when you are on vacation or completely removed from work-related activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve been truly “completely removed” from it lately. That’s part of the problem, I think. Even when I’m not physically in the lab, my head is still there — the experiment, the grant call, the safety meeting, the data, some odd detail I can’t let go of.\n\nSo if you mean, like, on an actual vacation where there’s no email, no project, no equipment, no students, no expectation that I’m making decisions… I honestly can’t give you a clean example right now. I haven’t really had that kind of separation.\n\nWhat I can say is that the focus problem is clearest when there’s something I’m supposed to be tracking carefully. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know the main thing matters, I know people are depending on me to follow it, and then something tiny catches me — a pump vibration in the background, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some strange peak from an old spectrum — and then it’s like that becomes the only thing I can think about. I go after it for hours, pulling old files or trying to make connections, and meanwhile the actual thing everyone needed from me just falls apart.\n\nSo… vacation, I’m not sure. But when I’m around anything connected to the work, even indirectly, I have a very hard time keeping my mind where it’s supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity prior to the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know how to answer that very confidently. Before age 12 feels like a long time ago, and I don’t have a clear memory of being described that way — like significantly inattentive or hyperactive. I mean, I can remember being a child and sometimes getting absorbed in things, or being distracted like any kid, but I don’t know that it rose to the level you’re asking about.\n\nI don’t want to say “no” too strongly, because maybe a parent or teacher would remember something I don’t, but from my own memory, nothing stands out as a major pattern before middle school. I don’t remember being constantly in trouble for not sitting still or not finishing things or acting impulsively. If there were issues, they weren’t obvious enough to me that I can point to them now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Not just making a note to check later, but actually losing hours to it, digging through old files or trying to connect it to something else, and meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me just… falls apart.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether everyday tasks get completed, I’d say the problem is more that whatever I’m supposed to be doing gets displaced. The thing in front of me gets hijacked by some other detail that feels urgent or meaningful. And by the time I come back to what I was supposed to finish, I’ve either missed the window or someone else has had to patch it together.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and restlessness present when you are on vacation or completely removed from work-related activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve been truly “completely removed” from it lately. That’s part of the problem, I think. Even when I’m not physically in the lab, my head is still there — the experiment, the grant call, the safety meeting, the data, some odd detail I can’t let go of.\n\nSo if you mean, like, on an actual vacation where there’s no email, no project, no equipment, no students, no expectation that I’m making decisions… I honestly can’t give you a clean example right now. I haven’t really had that kind of separation.\n\nWhat I can say is that the focus problem is clearest when there’s something I’m supposed to be tracking carefully. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know the main thing matters, I know people are depending on me to follow it, and then something tiny catches me — a pump vibration in the background, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some strange peak from an old spectrum — and then it’s like that becomes the only thing I can think about. I go after it for hours, pulling old files or trying to make connections, and meanwhile the actual thing everyone needed from me just falls apart.\n\nSo… vacation, I’m not sure. But when I’m around anything connected to the work, even indirectly, I have a very hard time keeping my mind where it’s supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity prior to the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know how to answer that very confidently. Before age 12 feels like a long time ago, and I don’t have a clear memory of being described that way — like significantly inattentive or hyperactive. I mean, I can remember being a child and sometimes getting absorbed in things, or being distracted like any kid, but I don’t know that it rose to the level you’re asking about.\n\nI don’t want to say “no” too strongly, because maybe a parent or teacher would remember something I don’t, but from my own memory, nothing stands out as a major pattern before middle school. I don’t remember being constantly in trouble for not sitting still or not finishing things or acting impulsively. If there were issues, they weren’t obvious enough to me that I can point to them now.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid your parents or teachers ever express concerns about your ability to focus or sit still when you were a child?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 42 / physical_scientist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I don’t have a clear memory of my parents or teachers sitting me down, or telling me there was some serious problem with focus or sitting still. At least nothing that stands out in the way you’re asking.

There may have been ordinary comments, you know — “pay attention,” or “stop daydreaming,” or whatever adults say to children — but I can’t honestly separate that from normal childhood stuff. I don’t remember conferences or evaluations or repeated complaints about me being unable to sit still. If that happened, it didn’t leave a strong impression on me.

I’d probably have to ask my parents, honestly. From my own memory, I can’t give you a solid yes.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m… I’m really not functioning the way I need to at work. I’m a physical scientist, so my whole job depends on being able to hold a line of thought through an experiment or a meeting or a grant call, and lately I just can’t keep my mind on the actual thing in front of me.\n\nLike, we’ll be in a safety meeting and I’m supposed to be tracking what everyone needs from me, and then I notice some tiny pump vibration in the lab, or a student makes a side comment that isn’t even the point, or I remember some weird peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly that’s all I can think about. I’ll go off chasing it for hours—digging through files, checking equipment, following some tangent—while the actual experiment or the deadline or the thing everyone is waiting on from me just kind of falls apart.\n\nIt’s not just annoying distraction. It feels like my attention gets yanked away, and once it’s gone, I can’t steer it back where it’s supposed to be. That’s what scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. It’s not just tapping my foot or shifting around in a chair, though I do that too. It’s more like if I’m sitting still, I feel this pressure building, like I’m wasting time or missing the one window where I could fix everything.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is there, sometimes way too early, and I’m already moving from thing to thing — rewriting protocols, rearranging the instrument schedule, checking calibration notes, starting analyses that probably could have waited. I’ll have three or four things open at once and be emailing collaborators in different countries because I suddenly feel like all of it needs to happen right now.\n\nAnd if I try to just sit at my desk and focus on one thing, it feels almost physically uncomfortable. Like sitting still is impossible because there’s too much to do and my body is sort of pushing me forward before I’ve even decided what I’m doing next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s wildly impulsive, if that makes sense. Like, I’m not usually doing things just for the thrill of it or because I want to be reckless.\n\nBut there are definitely times when I act before I’ve really thought through the whole chain of consequences. It’s more like I get this sudden certainty that something has to be done immediately, and then I’m already doing it before I’ve slowed down enough to ask whether it’s a good idea or whether anyone else needed to weigh in. Sometimes I only realize afterward, when things have already started moving, that I may have skipped a few steps.\n\nSo… yes, maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive in the moment. At the time it feels urgent, or obvious, or like waiting would be the irresponsible thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes. Overwhelmed is probably the better word than anxious, although I do get anxious once I realize how far off track I’ve gone.\n\nIt’s like… I’ll be in the middle of something that actually matters — a grant call, or a safety meeting, or an experiment where people are depending on me to follow what’s happening — and then some tiny thing grabs me. A pump vibration that sounds slightly wrong, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some strange peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly that becomes the only thing my brain wants to deal with.\n\nAnd I know, somewhere in the back of my mind, that I’m supposed to stay with the meeting or the actual experiment, but I can’t seem to make myself do it. I’ll go chasing the tangent for hours sometimes, pulling old files, checking equipment, looking through notes, and then the thing everyone actually needed from me is just… not done. Or I’ve missed the important part of the discussion.\n\nThat’s when I feel overwhelmed — when I see the consequences piling up and realize I couldn’t keep my attention where it was supposed to be. It feels humiliating, honestly, because my job depends on being able to think clearly and stay with a problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: It’s been about seven months now, I think. It started around the time I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just the project being bigger and more complicated than what I’d been managing before — different labs, different instruments, everyone’s schedules and data formats not quite lining up — so it seemed reasonable that I’d feel keyed up and pulled in too many directions. But looking back, that’s really when this became noticeable, not just being busy but feeling unable to settle, unable to stay with one thread without jumping to another.\n\nSo… about seven months. Since that project started being mine, basically.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or find it difficult to manage your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I wouldn’t have called them “mood swings” at first, because it didn’t feel like I was swinging randomly. It felt like the lab was finally moving at the speed it should have been moving all along. Like everything was lit up, almost electrified, and I could see what needed to happen so clearly.\n\nBut the emotional part has been hard to manage. For months now I’ve had this feeling of being intensely driven, like there’s this momentum and everyone should just get on board. And then if someone questions the plan, or asks whether we should slow down, or wants another meeting before acting, I can feel myself get irritated so fast. Not just mildly annoyed — I snap. I cut people off, or my tone gets sharp, and I’ll say things in a way that makes it sound like they’re being stupid for not seeing what I see.\n\nAnd I don’t like admitting that. I’ve noticed students avoiding my office, or hesitating before they bring something to me, like they’re bracing for how I’ll react. That feels awful, because I don’t want to be someone people are afraid to talk to. But in the moment, when they’re slowing things down, it feels almost unbearable. Like they’re dragging the whole lab backward.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personal relationships since you started experiencing these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly at work, though it feels personal because the lab has been such a big part of my life and identity.\n\nThings have changed with people who used to trust me without much second-guessing. My department has had to step in on decisions I normally would have handled myself — scheduling, priorities, who needed to do what and when. I used to be the person who kept all of that straight, or at least I thought I was. Lately I’ve missed things that should have been obvious, or I’ll focus hard on one detail and lose track of the larger priority, and then the schedule gets confused or people are waiting on me for direction.\n\nAnd it’s not just embarrassing, it’s strained the relationships. My students and staff don’t come to me as easily, and my colleagues have started checking behind me in ways they didn’t used to. I can tell they’re not just being helpful; they’re worried the project could get off track, or that I might miss something connected to lab safety expectations. That’s… hard to say out loud. I’ve always taken safety and supervision seriously.\n\nSo yes, the relationships have changed. There’s less trust, I think. More caution around me. And I hate that, because these are people I’ve worked with for years, and now it feels like the department is having to protect the project from my mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced similar difficulties with focus, restlessness, or managing your emotions in your personal life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve been thinking about that, because it’s hard to separate “personal life” from work right now. Work has sort of expanded into everything — my evenings, weekends, what I’m thinking about when I’m supposed to be doing something else — so when you ask outside of work, I don’t have a clean answer.\n\nI haven’t noticed it in the same concrete way, if that makes sense. At work there are obvious examples: missed scheduling things, getting irritated with people, jumping between tasks. At home or outside the lab, there isn’t really the same structure where I can point to it and say, “There, that’s the same problem.” I don’t know if that means it isn’t happening, or if I’m just less aware of it because there aren’t students and colleagues reflecting it back at me.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clear yes the way I can with work. Most of what I’ve noticed has been tied to the project and the lab.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing everyday tasks at home, like chores or personal projects, due to your difficulty with focus or restlessness?\n\nPatient: At home… I’m not sure it shows up as “chores” in the ordinary way, like dishes or laundry, because I haven’t been paying very good attention to those categories lately. The clearest version of it is still tied to the lab, even when I’m not physically there.\n\nThe reason I came in is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll go chasing it. Not just making a note to check later, but actually losing hours to it, digging through old files or trying to connect it to something else, and meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me just… falls apart.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether everyday tasks get completed, I’d say the problem is more that whatever I’m supposed to be doing gets displaced. The thing in front of me gets hijacked by some other detail that feels urgent or meaningful. And by the time I come back to what I was supposed to finish, I’ve either missed the window or someone else has had to patch it together.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and restlessness present when you are on vacation or completely removed from work-related activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve been truly “completely removed” from it lately. Not recently, no. I mean, there have probably been stretches in my adult life where I was more functional than I am right now, but I came in because it’s gotten to where I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.

Like, I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s a specific thing I’m supposed to be tracking — what someone is asking from me, what decision needs to be made — and then I’ll notice something totally off to the side. A pump vibration, or a student makes some little side comment, or I remember some weird peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly my brain just grabs onto that. And I’m gone. Not physically gone, but mentally I’m already chasing it, making connections, thinking I should check this or that, and then hours can disappear.

And meanwhile the actual thing everyone needed from me is… just not done, or it falls apart because I didn’t stay with it. It’s not just ordinary distraction like checking my phone. It feels like my attention gets hijacked by whatever seems suddenly important or interesting, even if it’s not the priority at all.

So, completely free of that? I don’t think I can say that. There may have been better periods, but this is enough of a pattern now that it’s interfering with my work in a pretty serious way.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m… I’m really not functioning the way I need to at work. I’m a physical scientist, so my whole job depends on being able to hold a line of thought through an experiment or a meeting or a grant call, and lately I just can’t keep my mind on the actual thing in front of me.\n\nLike, we’ll be in a safety meeting and I’m supposed to be tracking what everyone needs from me, and then I notice some tiny pump vibration in the lab, or a student makes a side comment that isn’t even the point, or I remember some weird peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly that’s all I can think about. I’ll go off chasing it for hours—digging through files, checking equipment, following some tangent—while the actual experiment or the deadline or the thing everyone is waiting on from me just kind of falls apart.\n\nIt’s not just annoying distraction. It feels like my attention gets yanked away, and once it’s gone, I can’t steer it back where it’s supposed to be. That’s what scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. It’s not just tapping my foot or shifting around in a chair, though I do that too. It’s more like if I’m sitting still, I feel this pressure building, like I’m wasting time or missing the one window where I could fix everything.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is there, sometimes way too early, and I’m already moving from thing to thing — rewriting protocols, rearranging the instrument schedule, checking calibration notes, starting analyses that probably could have waited. I’ll have three or four things open at once and be emailing collaborators in different countries because I suddenly feel like all of it needs to happen right now.\n\nAnd if I try to just sit at my desk and focus on one thing, it feels almost physically uncomfortable. Like sitting still is impossible because there’s too much to do and my body is sort of pushing me forward before I’ve even decided what I’m doing next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s wildly impulsive, if that makes sense. Like, I’m not usually doing things just for the thrill of it or because I want to be reckless.\n\nBut there are definitely times when I act before I’ve really thought through the whole chain of consequences. It’s more like I get this sudden certainty that something has to be done immediately, and then I’m already doing it before I’ve slowed down enough to ask whether it’s a good idea or whether anyone else needed to weigh in. Sometimes I only realize afterward, when things have already started moving, that I may have skipped a few steps.\n\nSo… yes, maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive in the moment. At the time it feels urgent, or obvious, or like waiting would be the irresponsible thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes. Overwhelmed is probably the better word than anxious, although I do get anxious once I realize how far off track I’ve gone.\n\nIt’s like… I’ll be in the middle of something that actually matters — a grant call, or a safety meeting, or an experiment where people are depending on me to follow what’s happening — and then some tiny thing grabs me. A pump vibration that sounds slightly wrong, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some strange peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly that becomes the only thing my brain wants to deal with.\n\nAnd I know, somewhere in the back of my mind, that I’m supposed to stay with the meeting or the actual experiment, but I can’t seem to make myself do it. I’ll go chasing the tangent for hours sometimes, pulling old files, checking equipment, looking through notes, and then the thing everyone actually needed from me is just… not done. Or I’ve missed the important part of the discussion.\n\nThat’s when I feel overwhelmed — when I see the consequences piling up and realize I couldn’t keep my attention where it was supposed to be. It feels humiliating, honestly, because my job depends on being able to think clearly and stay with a problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: It’s been about seven months now, I think. It started around the time I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just the project being bigger and more complicated than what I’d been managing before — different labs, different instruments, everyone’s schedules and data formats not quite lining up — so it seemed reasonable that I’d feel keyed up and pulled in too many directions. But looking back, that’s really when this became noticeable, not just being busy but feeling unable to settle, unable to stay with one thread without jumping to another.\n\nSo… about seven months. Since that project started being mine, basically.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or find it difficult to manage your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I wouldn’t have called them “mood swings” at first, because it didn’t feel like I was swinging randomly. It felt like the lab was finally moving at the speed it should have been moving all along. Like everything was lit up, almost electrified, and I could see what needed to happen so clearly.\n\nBut the emotional part has been hard to manage. For months now I’ve had this feeling of being intensely driven, like there’s this momentum and everyone should just get on board. And then if someone questions the plan, or asks whether we should slow down, or wants another meeting before acting, I can feel myself get irritated so fast. Not just mildly annoyed — I snap. I cut people off, or my tone gets sharp, and I’ll say things in a way that makes it sound like they’re being stupid for not seeing what I see.\n\nAnd I don’t like admitting that. I’ve noticed students avoiding my office, or hesitating before they bring something to me, like they’re bracing for how I’ll react. That feels awful, because I don’t want to be someone people are afraid to talk to. But in the moment, when they’re slowing things down, it feels almost unbearable. Like they’re dragging the whole lab backward.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personal relationships since you started experiencing these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly at work, though it feels personal because the lab has been such a big part of my life and identity.\n\nThings have changed with people who used to trust me without much second-guessing. My department has had to step in on decisions I normally would have handled myself — scheduling, priorities, who needed to do what and when. I used to be the person who kept all of that straight, or at least I thought I was. Lately I’ve missed things that should have been obvious, or I’ll focus hard on one detail and lose track of the larger priority, and then the schedule gets confused or people are waiting on me for direction.\n\nAnd it’s not just embarrassing, it’s strained the relationships. My students and staff don’t come to me as easily, and my colleagues have started checking behind me in ways they didn’t used to. I can tell they’re not just being helpful; they’re worried the project could get off track, or that I might miss something connected to lab safety expectations. That’s… hard to say out loud. I’ve always taken safety and supervision seriously.\n\nSo yes, the relationships have changed. There’s less trust, I think. More caution around me. And I hate that, because these are people I’ve worked with for years, and now it feels like the department is having to protect the project from my mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced similar difficulties with focus, restlessness, or managing your emotions in your personal life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve been thinking about that, because it’s hard to separate “personal life” from work right now. Work has sort of expanded into everything — my evenings, weekends, what I’m thinking about when I’m supposed to be doing something else — so when you ask outside of work, I don’t have a clean answer.\n\nI haven’t noticed it in the same concrete way, if that makes sense. At work there are obvious examples: missed scheduling things, getting irritated with people, jumping between tasks. At home or outside the lab, there isn’t really the same structure where I can point to it and say, “There, that’s the same problem.” I don’t know if that means it isn’t happening, or if I’m just less aware of it because there aren’t students and colleagues reflecting it back at me.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clear yes the way I can with work. Most of what I’ve noticed has been tied to the project and the lab.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing everyday tasks at home, like chores or personal projects, due to your difficulty with focus or restlessness?\n\nPatient: At home… I’m not sure it shows up as “chores” in the ordinary way, like dishes or laundry, because I haven’t been paying very good attention to those categories lately. The clearest version of it is still tied to the lab, even when I’m not physically there.\n\nThe reason I came in is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call, or in a safety meeting where I really do need to be tracking the main point, and then I’ll notice something — like a pump vibration in the background, or a student making some side comment, or I’ll remember some odd peak from an old spectrum — and suddenly that becomes the most important thing in the world. I’ll go chasing it. Not just making a note to check later, but actually losing hours to it, digging through old files or trying to connect it to something else, and meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me just… falls apart.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether everyday tasks get completed, I’d say the problem is more that whatever I’m supposed to be doing gets displaced. The thing in front of me gets hijacked by some other detail that feels urgent or meaningful. And by the time I come back to what I was supposed to finish, I’ve either missed the window or someone else has had to patch it together.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and restlessness present when you are on vacation or completely removed from work-related activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve been truly “completely removed” from it lately. That’s part of the problem, I think. Even when I’m not physically in the lab, my head is still there — the experiment, the grant call, the safety meeting, the data, some odd detail I can’t let go of.\n\nSo if you mean, like, on an actual vacation where there’s no email, no project, no equipment, no students, no expectation that I’m making decisions… I honestly can’t give you a clean example right now. I haven’t really had that kind of separation.\n\nWhat I can say is that the focus problem is clearest when there’s something I’m supposed to be tracking carefully. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know the main thing matters, I know people are depending on me to follow it, and then something tiny catches me — a pump vibration in the background, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some strange peak from an old spectrum — and then it’s like that becomes the only thing I can think about. I go after it for hours, pulling old files or trying to make connections, and meanwhile the actual thing everyone needed from me just falls apart.\n\nSo… vacation, I’m not sure. But when I’m around anything connected to the work, even indirectly, I have a very hard time keeping my mind where it’s supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity prior to the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know how to answer that very confidently. Before age 12 feels like a long time ago, and I don’t have a clear memory of being described that way — like significantly inattentive or hyperactive. I mean, I can remember being a child and sometimes getting absorbed in things, or being distracted like any kid, but I don’t know that it rose to the level you’re asking about.\n\nI don’t want to say “no” too strongly, because maybe a parent or teacher would remember something I don’t, but from my own memory, nothing stands out as a major pattern before middle school. I don’t remember being constantly in trouble for not sitting still or not finishing things or acting impulsively. If there were issues, they weren’t obvious enough to me that I can point to them now.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever express concerns about your ability to focus or sit still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a clear memory of my parents or teachers sitting me down, or telling me there was some serious problem with focus or sitting still. At least nothing that stands out in the way you’re asking.\n\nThere may have been ordinary comments, you know — “pay attention,” or “stop daydreaming,” or whatever adults say to children — but I can’t honestly separate that from normal childhood stuff. I don’t remember conferences or evaluations or repeated complaints about me being unable to sit still. If that happened, it didn’t leave a strong impression on me.\n\nI’d probably have to ask my parents, honestly. From my own memory, I can’t give you a solid yes.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any symptoms like difficulty focusing or restlessness before you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m sort of circling the same answer, but I really don’t have a clear memory of that. Before I was a teenager is just… fuzzy in terms of those kinds of patterns.\n\nI can remember being a kid and having normal kid moments — getting bored, daydreaming, needing reminders — but I don’t remember it being a defined problem, like “she can’t focus” or “she’s always restless.” Nothing that stands out as a repeated issue or something people were alarmed about.\n\nSo my honest answer is: I’m not sure. From my own memory, I can’t point to symptoms like that before I was a teenager, at least not in a way that feels significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your adult life where you felt completely free of symptoms like difficulty focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Not recently, no. I mean, there have probably been stretches in my adult life where I was more functional than I am right now, but I came in because it’s gotten to where I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s a specific thing I’m supposed to be tracking — what someone is asking from me, what decision needs to be made — and then I’ll notice something totally off to the side. A pump vibration, or a student makes some little side comment, or I remember some weird peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly my brain just grabs onto that. And I’m gone. Not physically gone, but mentally I’m already chasing it, making connections, thinking I should check this or that, and then hours can disappear.\n\nAnd meanwhile the actual thing everyone needed from me is… just not done, or it falls apart because I didn’t stay with it. It’s not just ordinary distraction like checking my phone. It feels like my attention gets hijacked by whatever seems suddenly important or interesting, even if it’s not the priority at all.\n\nSo, completely free of that? I don’t think I can say that. There may have been better periods, but this is enough of a pattern now that it’s interfering with my work in a pretty serious way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

For months now I’ve had this feeling of being intensely driven, like there’s this momentum and everyone should just get on board. I cut people off, or my tone gets sharp, and I’ll say things in a way that makes it sound like they’re being stupid for not seeing what I see.\n\nAnd I don’t like admitting that. I’ve noticed students avoiding my office, or hesitating before they bring something to me, like they’re bracing for how I’ll react. My department has had to step in on decisions I normally would have handled myself — scheduling, priorities, who needed to do what and when. I used to be the person who kept all of that straight, or at least I thought I was. Lately I’ve missed things that should have been obvious, or I’ll focus hard on one detail and lose track of the larger priority, and then the schedule gets confused or people are waiting on me for direction.\n\nAnd it’s not just embarrassing, it’s strained the relationships. My students and staff don’t come to me as easily, and my colleagues have started checking behind me in ways they didn’t used to. I can tell they’re not just being helpful; they’re worried the project could get off track, or that I might miss something connected to lab safety expectations. That’s… hard to say out loud. I’ve always taken safety and supervision seriously.\n\nSo yes, the relationships have changed. There’s less trust, I think. More caution around me. Before I was a teenager is just… fuzzy in terms of those kinds of patterns.\n\nI can remember being a kid and having normal kid moments — getting bored, daydreaming, needing reminders — but I don’t remember it being a defined problem, like “she can’t focus” or “she’s always restless.” Nothing that stands out as a repeated issue or something people were alarmed about.\n\nSo my honest answer is: I’m not sure. From my own memory, I can’t point to symptoms like that before I was a teenager, at least not in a way that feels significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your adult life where you felt completely free of symptoms like difficulty focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Not recently, no. I mean, there have probably been stretches in my adult life where I was more functional than I am right now, but I came in because it’s gotten to where I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s a specific thing I’m supposed to be tracking — what someone is asking from me, what decision needs to be made — and then I’ll notice something totally off to the side. A pump vibration, or a student makes some little side comment, or I remember some weird peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly my brain just grabs onto that. And I’m gone. Not physically gone, but mentally I’m already chasing it, making connections, thinking I should check this or that, and then hours can disappear.\n\nAnd meanwhile the actual thing everyone needed from me is… just not done, or it falls apart because I didn’t stay with it. It’s not just ordinary distraction like checking my phone. It feels like my attention gets hijacked by whatever seems suddenly important or interesting, even if it’s not the priority at all.\n\nSo, completely free of that? I don’t think I can say that. There may have been better periods, but this is enough of a pattern now that it’s interfering with my work in a pretty serious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any other mental health conditions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — not formally, anyway. I mean, no one has sat me down and said, “You have this specific other diagnosis,” at least not that I remember.\n\nI’ve had people use general language over the years, like stress, burnout, that kind of thing, but I don’t know that that counts as a diagnosis. And I’m a little careful answering because I don’t want to accidentally overstate something just because a word was mentioned casually in a conversation.\n\nSo as far as I know, no, I haven’t been diagnosed with any other mental health condition.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I cut people off, or my tone gets sharp, and I’ll say things in a way that makes it sound like they’re being stupid for not seeing what I see.\n\nAnd I don’t like admitting that. That feels awful, because I don’t want to be someone people are afraid to talk to. But in the moment, when they’re slowing things down, it feels almost unbearable. My department has had to step in on decisions I normally would have handled myself — scheduling, priorities, who needed to do what and when. I used to be the person who kept all of that straight, or at least I thought I was. Lately I’ve missed things that should have been obvious, or I’ll focus hard on one detail and lose track of the larger priority, and then the schedule gets confused or people are waiting on me for direction.\n\nAnd it’s not just embarrassing, it’s strained the relationships. My students and staff don’t come to me as easily, and my colleagues have started checking behind me in ways they didn’t used to. And I’m a little careful answering because I don’t want to accidentally overstate something just because a word was mentioned casually in a conversation.\n\nSo as far as I know, no, I haven’t been diagnosed with any other mental health condition.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced difficulties with focus or restlessness in social situations or when you're alone, outside of work or lab-related activities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 42 / physical_scientist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Manic_Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can't keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore—during a grant call or safety meeting I'll notice a pump vibration, a student's side comment, or some odd peak in an old spectrum and suddenly I'm off chasing that for hours while the thing everyone needs from me falls apart.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… I’m not sure how to separate it cleanly from work, because that’s where it shows up most obviously and where there are consequences I can point to.

Like, the reason I came in is really because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. That sounds very lab-specific, I know, but it’s the best example because it’s so concrete. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know I’m supposed to be listening for a decision or an action item, and then something small catches me — a pump vibration, or a student making some little side comment, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum — and suddenly I’m not with the meeting anymore. I’m chasing that thought. It feels important, urgent almost, and then I can lose hours to it while the original thing everyone needed from me just… falls apart.

Outside of work, I don’t have as clean an example. I mean, socially, I can get pulled off by some detail or thought and lose the thread, but I notice it less because there isn’t always a specific experiment or deadline proving that I’ve gone off-track. When I’m alone, it can happen as that same kind of mental detour — one thing reminds me of another, and then I’m deep into it before I realize I’ve abandoned what I meant to be doing.

But the clearest, most damaging version is definitely the lab/work version. That’s where I can see it happening in real time and then see the consequences afterward.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m… I’m really not functioning the way I need to at work. I’m a physical scientist, so my whole job depends on being able to hold a line of thought through an experiment or a meeting or a grant call, and lately I just can’t keep my mind on the actual thing in front of me.\n\nLike, we’ll be in a safety meeting and I’m supposed to be tracking what everyone needs from me, and then I notice some tiny pump vibration in the lab, or a student makes a side comment that isn’t even the point, or I remember some weird peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly that’s all I can think about. I’ll go off chasing it for hours—digging through files, checking equipment, following some tangent—while the actual experiment or the deadline or the thing everyone is waiting on from me just kind of falls apart.\n\nIt’s not just annoying distraction. It feels like my attention gets yanked away, and once it’s gone, I can’t steer it back where it’s supposed to be. That’s what scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. It’s not just tapping my foot or shifting around in a chair, though I do that too. It’s more like if I’m sitting still, I feel this pressure building, like I’m wasting time or missing the one window where I could fix everything.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is there, sometimes way too early, and I’m already moving from thing to thing — rewriting protocols, rearranging the instrument schedule, checking calibration notes, starting analyses that probably could have waited. I’ll have three or four things open at once and be emailing collaborators in different countries because I suddenly feel like all of it needs to happen right now.\n\nAnd if I try to just sit at my desk and focus on one thing, it feels almost physically uncomfortable. Like sitting still is impossible because there’s too much to do and my body is sort of pushing me forward before I’ve even decided what I’m doing next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s wildly impulsive, if that makes sense. Like, I’m not usually doing things just for the thrill of it or because I want to be reckless.\n\nBut there are definitely times when I act before I’ve really thought through the whole chain of consequences. It’s more like I get this sudden certainty that something has to be done immediately, and then I’m already doing it before I’ve slowed down enough to ask whether it’s a good idea or whether anyone else needed to weigh in. Sometimes I only realize afterward, when things have already started moving, that I may have skipped a few steps.\n\nSo… yes, maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive in the moment. At the time it feels urgent, or obvious, or like waiting would be the irresponsible thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes. Overwhelmed is probably the better word than anxious, although I do get anxious once I realize how far off track I’ve gone.\n\nIt’s like… I’ll be in the middle of something that actually matters — a grant call, or a safety meeting, or an experiment where people are depending on me to follow what’s happening — and then some tiny thing grabs me. A pump vibration that sounds slightly wrong, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some strange peak from an old spectrum, and suddenly that becomes the only thing my brain wants to deal with.\n\nAnd I know, somewhere in the back of my mind, that I’m supposed to stay with the meeting or the actual experiment, but I can’t seem to make myself do it. I’ll go chasing the tangent for hours sometimes, pulling old files, checking equipment, looking through notes, and then the thing everyone actually needed from me is just… not done. Or I’ve missed the important part of the discussion.\n\nThat’s when I feel overwhelmed — when I see the consequences piling up and realize I couldn’t keep my attention where it was supposed to be. It feels humiliating, honestly, because my job depends on being able to think clearly and stay with a problem.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: It’s been about seven months now, I think. It started around the time I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just the project being bigger and more complicated than what I’d been managing before — different labs, different instruments, everyone’s schedules and data formats not quite lining up — so it seemed reasonable that I’d feel keyed up and pulled in too many directions. But looking back, that’s really when this became noticeable, not just being busy but feeling unable to settle, unable to stay with one thread without jumping to another.\n\nSo… about seven months. Since that project started being mine, basically.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or find it difficult to manage your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I wouldn’t have called them “mood swings” at first, because it didn’t feel like I was swinging randomly. It felt like the lab was finally moving at the speed it should have been moving all along. Like everything was lit up, almost electrified, and I could see what needed to happen so clearly.\n\nBut the emotional part has been hard to manage. For months now I’ve had this feeling of being intensely driven, like there’s this momentum and everyone should just get on board. And then if someone questions the plan, or asks whether we should slow down, or wants another meeting before acting, I can feel myself get irritated so fast. Not just mildly annoyed — I snap. I cut people off, or my tone gets sharp, and I’ll say things in a way that makes it sound like they’re being stupid for not seeing what I see.\n\nAnd I don’t like admitting that. I’ve noticed students avoiding my office, or hesitating before they bring something to me, like they’re bracing for how I’ll react. That feels awful, because I don’t want to be someone people are afraid to talk to. But in the moment, when they’re slowing things down, it feels almost unbearable. Like they’re dragging the whole lab backward.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personal relationships since you started experiencing these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly at work, though it feels personal because the lab has been such a big part of my life and identity.\n\nThings have changed with people who used to trust me without much second-guessing. My department has had to step in on decisions I normally would have handled myself — scheduling, priorities, who needed to do what and when. I used to be the person who kept all of that straight, or at least I thought I was. Lately I’ve missed things that should have been obvious, or I’ll focus hard on one detail and lose track of the larger priority, and then the schedule gets confused or people are waiting on me for direction.\n\nAnd it’s not just embarrassing, it’s strained the relationships. My students and staff don’t come to me as easily, and my colleagues have started checking behind me in ways they didn’t used to. I can tell they’re not just being helpful; they’re worried the project could get off track, or that I might miss something connected to lab safety expectations. That’s… hard to say out loud. I’ve always taken safety and supervision seriously.\n\nSo yes, the relationships have changed. There’s less trust, I think. More caution around me. And I hate that, because these are people I’ve worked with for years, and now it feels like the department is having to protect the project from my mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced similar difficulties with focus, restlessness, or managing your emotions in your personal life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve been thinking about that, because it’s hard to separate “personal life” from work right now. Work has sort of expanded into everything — my evenings, weekends, what I’m thinking about when I’m supposed to be doing something else — so when you ask outside of work, I don’t have a clean answer.\n\nI haven’t noticed it in the same concrete way, if that makes sense. At work there are obvious examples: missed scheduling things, getting irritated with people, jumping between tasks. At home or outside the lab, there isn’t really the same structure where I can point to it and say, “There, that’s the same problem.” I don’t know if that means it isn’t happening, or if I’m just less aware of it because there aren’t students and colleagues reflecting it back at me.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clear yes the way I can with work. Most of what I’ve noticed has been tied to the project and the lab.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing everyday tasks at home, like chores or personal projects, due to your difficulty with focus or restlessness?\n\nPatient: At home… I’m not sure it shows up as “chores” in the ordinary way, like dishes or laundry, because I haven’t been paying very good attention to those categories lately. The clearest version of it is still tied to the lab, even when I’m not physically there.\n\nThe reason I came in is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call, or in a safety meeting where I really do need to be tracking the main point, and then I’ll notice something — like a pump vibration in the background, or a student making some side comment, or I’ll remember some odd peak from an old spectrum — and suddenly that becomes the most important thing in the world. I’ll go chasing it. Not just making a note to check later, but actually losing hours to it, digging through old files or trying to connect it to something else, and meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me just… falls apart.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether everyday tasks get completed, I’d say the problem is more that whatever I’m supposed to be doing gets displaced. The thing in front of me gets hijacked by some other detail that feels urgent or meaningful. And by the time I come back to what I was supposed to finish, I’ve either missed the window or someone else has had to patch it together.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and restlessness present when you are on vacation or completely removed from work-related activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve been truly “completely removed” from it lately. That’s part of the problem, I think. Even when I’m not physically in the lab, my head is still there — the experiment, the grant call, the safety meeting, the data, some odd detail I can’t let go of.\n\nSo if you mean, like, on an actual vacation where there’s no email, no project, no equipment, no students, no expectation that I’m making decisions… I honestly can’t give you a clean example right now. I haven’t really had that kind of separation.\n\nWhat I can say is that the focus problem is clearest when there’s something I’m supposed to be tracking carefully. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know the main thing matters, I know people are depending on me to follow it, and then something tiny catches me — a pump vibration in the background, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some strange peak from an old spectrum — and then it’s like that becomes the only thing I can think about. I go after it for hours, pulling old files or trying to make connections, and meanwhile the actual thing everyone needed from me just falls apart.\n\nSo… vacation, I’m not sure. But when I’m around anything connected to the work, even indirectly, I have a very hard time keeping my mind where it’s supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity prior to the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know how to answer that very confidently. Before age 12 feels like a long time ago, and I don’t have a clear memory of being described that way — like significantly inattentive or hyperactive. I mean, I can remember being a child and sometimes getting absorbed in things, or being distracted like any kid, but I don’t know that it rose to the level you’re asking about.\n\nI don’t want to say “no” too strongly, because maybe a parent or teacher would remember something I don’t, but from my own memory, nothing stands out as a major pattern before middle school. I don’t remember being constantly in trouble for not sitting still or not finishing things or acting impulsively. If there were issues, they weren’t obvious enough to me that I can point to them now.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever express concerns about your ability to focus or sit still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a clear memory of my parents or teachers sitting me down, or telling me there was some serious problem with focus or sitting still. At least nothing that stands out in the way you’re asking.\n\nThere may have been ordinary comments, you know — “pay attention,” or “stop daydreaming,” or whatever adults say to children — but I can’t honestly separate that from normal childhood stuff. I don’t remember conferences or evaluations or repeated complaints about me being unable to sit still. If that happened, it didn’t leave a strong impression on me.\n\nI’d probably have to ask my parents, honestly. From my own memory, I can’t give you a solid yes.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any symptoms like difficulty focusing or restlessness before you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m sort of circling the same answer, but I really don’t have a clear memory of that. Before I was a teenager is just… fuzzy in terms of those kinds of patterns.\n\nI can remember being a kid and having normal kid moments — getting bored, daydreaming, needing reminders — but I don’t remember it being a defined problem, like “she can’t focus” or “she’s always restless.” Nothing that stands out as a repeated issue or something people were alarmed about.\n\nSo my honest answer is: I’m not sure. From my own memory, I can’t point to symptoms like that before I was a teenager, at least not in a way that feels significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your adult life where you felt completely free of symptoms like difficulty focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Not recently, no. I mean, there have probably been stretches in my adult life where I was more functional than I am right now, but I came in because it’s gotten to where I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s a specific thing I’m supposed to be tracking — what someone is asking from me, what decision needs to be made — and then I’ll notice something totally off to the side. A pump vibration, or a student makes some little side comment, or I remember some weird peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly my brain just grabs onto that. And I’m gone. Not physically gone, but mentally I’m already chasing it, making connections, thinking I should check this or that, and then hours can disappear.\n\nAnd meanwhile the actual thing everyone needed from me is… just not done, or it falls apart because I didn’t stay with it. It’s not just ordinary distraction like checking my phone. It feels like my attention gets hijacked by whatever seems suddenly important or interesting, even if it’s not the priority at all.\n\nSo, completely free of that? I don’t think I can say that. There may have been better periods, but this is enough of a pattern now that it’s interfering with my work in a pretty serious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any other mental health conditions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — not formally, anyway. I mean, no one has sat me down and said, “You have this specific other diagnosis,” at least not that I remember.\n\nI’ve had people use general language over the years, like stress, burnout, that kind of thing, but I don’t know that that counts as a diagnosis. And I hate that, because these are people I’ve worked with for years, and now it feels like the department is having to protect the project from my mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced similar difficulties with focus, restlessness, or managing your emotions in your personal life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve been thinking about that, because it’s hard to separate “personal life” from work right now. Work has sort of expanded into everything — my evenings, weekends, what I’m thinking about when I’m supposed to be doing something else — so when you ask outside of work, I don’t have a clean answer.\n\nI haven’t noticed it in the same concrete way, if that makes sense. At work there are obvious examples: missed scheduling things, getting irritated with people, jumping between tasks. At home or outside the lab, there isn’t really the same structure where I can point to it and say, “There, that’s the same problem.” I don’t know if that means it isn’t happening, or if I’m just less aware of it because there aren’t students and colleagues reflecting it back at me.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clear yes the way I can with work. Most of what I’ve noticed has been tied to the project and the lab.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing everyday tasks at home, like chores or personal projects, due to your difficulty with focus or restlessness?\n\nPatient: At home… I’m not sure it shows up as “chores” in the ordinary way, like dishes or laundry, because I haven’t been paying very good attention to those categories lately. The clearest version of it is still tied to the lab, even when I’m not physically there.\n\nThe reason I came in is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call, or in a safety meeting where I really do need to be tracking the main point, and then I’ll notice something — like a pump vibration in the background, or a student making some side comment, or I’ll remember some odd peak from an old spectrum — and suddenly that becomes the most important thing in the world. I’ll go chasing it. Not just making a note to check later, but actually losing hours to it, digging through old files or trying to connect it to something else, and meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me just… falls apart.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether everyday tasks get completed, I’d say the problem is more that whatever I’m supposed to be doing gets displaced. The thing in front of me gets hijacked by some other detail that feels urgent or meaningful. And by the time I come back to what I was supposed to finish, I’ve either missed the window or someone else has had to patch it together.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and restlessness present when you are on vacation or completely removed from work-related activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve been truly “completely removed” from it lately. That’s part of the problem, I think. Even when I’m not physically in the lab, my head is still there — the experiment, the grant call, the safety meeting, the data, some odd detail I can’t let go of.\n\nSo if you mean, like, on an actual vacation where there’s no email, no project, no equipment, no students, no expectation that I’m making decisions… I honestly can’t give you a clean example right now. I haven’t really had that kind of separation.\n\nWhat I can say is that the focus problem is clearest when there’s something I’m supposed to be tracking carefully. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I know the main thing matters, I know people are depending on me to follow it, and then something tiny catches me — a pump vibration in the background, or a student’s side comment, or I’ll suddenly remember some strange peak from an old spectrum — and then it’s like that becomes the only thing I can think about. I go after it for hours, pulling old files or trying to make connections, and meanwhile the actual thing everyone needed from me just falls apart.\n\nSo… vacation, I’m not sure. But when I’m around anything connected to the work, even indirectly, I have a very hard time keeping my mind where it’s supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity prior to the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know how to answer that very confidently. Before age 12 feels like a long time ago, and I don’t have a clear memory of being described that way — like significantly inattentive or hyperactive. I mean, I can remember being a child and sometimes getting absorbed in things, or being distracted like any kid, but I don’t know that it rose to the level you’re asking about.\n\nI don’t want to say “no” too strongly, because maybe a parent or teacher would remember something I don’t, but from my own memory, nothing stands out as a major pattern before middle school. I don’t remember being constantly in trouble for not sitting still or not finishing things or acting impulsively. If there were issues, they weren’t obvious enough to me that I can point to them now.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever express concerns about your ability to focus or sit still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a clear memory of my parents or teachers sitting me down, or telling me there was some serious problem with focus or sitting still. At least nothing that stands out in the way you’re asking.\n\nThere may have been ordinary comments, you know — “pay attention,” or “stop daydreaming,” or whatever adults say to children — but I can’t honestly separate that from normal childhood stuff. I don’t remember conferences or evaluations or repeated complaints about me being unable to sit still. If that happened, it didn’t leave a strong impression on me.\n\nI’d probably have to ask my parents, honestly. From my own memory, I can’t give you a solid yes.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any symptoms like difficulty focusing or restlessness before you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m sort of circling the same answer, but I really don’t have a clear memory of that. Before I was a teenager is just… fuzzy in terms of those kinds of patterns.\n\nI can remember being a kid and having normal kid moments — getting bored, daydreaming, needing reminders — but I don’t remember it being a defined problem, like “she can’t focus” or “she’s always restless.” Nothing that stands out as a repeated issue or something people were alarmed about.\n\nSo my honest answer is: I’m not sure. From my own memory, I can’t point to symptoms like that before I was a teenager, at least not in a way that feels significant.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in your adult life where you felt completely free of symptoms like difficulty focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Not recently, no. I mean, there have probably been stretches in my adult life where I was more functional than I am right now, but I came in because it’s gotten to where I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s a specific thing I’m supposed to be tracking — what someone is asking from me, what decision needs to be made — and then I’ll notice something totally off to the side. A pump vibration, or a student makes some little side comment, or I remember some weird peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly my brain just grabs onto that. And I’m gone. Not physically gone, but mentally I’m already chasing it, making connections, thinking I should check this or that, and then hours can disappear.\n\nAnd meanwhile the actual thing everyone needed from me is… just not done, or it falls apart because I didn’t stay with it. It’s not just ordinary distraction like checking my phone. It feels like my attention gets hijacked by whatever seems suddenly important or interesting, even if it’s not the priority at all.\n\nSo, completely free of that? I don’t think I can say that. There may have been better periods, but this is enough of a pattern now that it’s interfering with my work in a pretty serious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any other mental health conditions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so — not formally, anyway. I mean, no one has sat me down and said, “You have this specific other diagnosis,” at least not that I remember.\n\nI’ve had people use general language over the years, like stress, burnout, that kind of thing, but I don’t know that that counts as a diagnosis. And I’m a little careful answering because I don’t want to accidentally overstate something just because a word was mentioned casually in a conversation.\n\nSo as far as I know, no, I haven’t been diagnosed with any other mental health condition.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus or restlessness in social situations or when you're alone, outside of work or lab-related activities?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m not sure how to separate it cleanly from work, because that’s where it shows up most obviously and where there are consequences I can point to.\n\nLike, the reason I came in is really because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.
